========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came in because... I just can't seem to shake this really heavy, hopeless feeling lately. It's sort of like... well, I’m a civil engineer, so I think about structural integrity and load-bearing a lot. Actually, the new overpass on I-95 they just finished? I worked on the preliminary calculations for that a few years ago. My ex-husband used to absolutely hate driving under overpasses, he always swore they were going to cave in on his car. Which is ironic, I guess, because right now it feels like everything I built for myself after the divorce actually has collapsed, and I’m just... sitting under it. Like I'm trapped under all the rubble and I can't lift it. 

Oh, by the way, did you notice the hairline crack in the drywall out in your waiting room? It’s probably just normal building settlement, but I couldn't stop staring at it while I was sitting out there. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because of that heavy feeling.

It's been... I'd say it's been hanging over me for about a month now. It really started getting bad right around all the financial mess after the divorce. deciding.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks no one asked me to take on, and pacing around my apartment when there was What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can't seem to shake this really heavy, hopeless feeling lately. It's sort of like... well, I’m a civil engineer, so I think about structural integrity and load-bearing a lot. Actually, the new overpass on I-95 they just finished? I worked on the preliminary calculations for that a few years ago. My ex-husband used to absolutely hate driving under overpasses, he always swore they were going to cave in on his car. They used to machine the brass so precisely, long before AutoCAD or CNC machines were even a concept. \n\nActually, my ex-husband knocked it off my workbench once. He was trying to reach a golf club or something in the garage. He played golf every single Sunday with his boss, which was always just an excuse to drink scotch at 10 AM, if you ask me. He had this terrible swing, too, completely off-balance. The physics of his stance were just a total disaster, no center of gravity whatsoever. But um... yeah, the transit level got a little dented when it fell. I was going to try and buff it out, but... I don't know. What did you ask me? Something about my hobbies? I guess... yeah, I guess I have. I haven't touched the transit level in weeks. It's just sitting there on the workbench next to all my polishing cloths. I used to buy those microfiber cloths in bulk from this industrial supply catalog. They're actually meant for cleaning optical lenses on satellites, which is fascinating if you think about it. The tolerances for space-grade optics are so tight that even a microscopic speck of dust can ruin a million-dollar image. 

My ex-husband thought it was ridiculous that I spent so much on cleaning cloths. He used to just wipe his glasses on his shirt, which drove me absolutely insane. You can literally see the micro-scratches forming on the anti-reflective coating when you do that. He had these horrible wire-rimmed frames, too. They made him look like a low-budget accountant from the 90s. I told him a hundred times to get thicker frames, maybe something in a tortoiseshell, to balance out his jawline, but he never listened. He was stubborn like that about everything. 

But um... what were we talking about? The surveying equipment? Yeah, I just... I look at it, and I know I should want to fix it, but I just don't have the energy to even pick up the polishing cloth.

Weight? Um... I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale. I actually threw my scale out a few months ago because it was one of those smart scales that syncs to your phone via Bluetooth, and the app was just terribly designed. The user interface was a nightmare. They used this awful neon green font on a white background, which completely violates basic accessibility standards for contrast ratios. I don't know who their software engineers were, but they clearly didn't do any beta testing with actual humans. 

My ex-husband loved that scale, though. He was obsessed with tracking his BMI, even though BMI is a completely outdated metric that doesn't account for muscle mass or bone density. Not that he had much muscle mass to speak of. He tried to get into CrossFit for about three weeks, bought all the expensive gear, the specialized shoes with the flat soles, the knee sleeves... and then he pulled a hamstring doing a box jump because his form was just atrocious. I tried to explain the biomechanics of a proper landing to him, how you need to absorb the kinetic energy through your hips and knees, but he just got defensive. 

So yeah, I threw the scale away after he moved out. I haven't really noticed if my clothes fit differently or anything. I just... I haven't been eating much, I guess, but I don't know about my weight.

honestly believed I could outthink the whole mess, fix my finances, save a couple of troubled projects at work, and maybe launch myself as some kind of sustainability expert all at once.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: For several nights I slept maybe two or three hours and still felt sharp enough to keep working, like sleep was an inefficient use of time.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: In meetings my attention kept jumping from the HVAC noise to someone’s pen tapping to a random idea about a site layout, and I’d lose the thread of what we were deciding.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks no one asked me to take on, and pacing around my apartment when there was nothing useful left to do.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I was making questionable calls at work and falling apart in meetings, and my supervisor had to take me off active project decisions. At home I stopped keeping up with basic routines and ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The out-of-control shift from being intensely wired to crashing has been going on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depression has been hanging over me for about a month, starting around the financial mess after the divorce.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I... I really couldn't tell you. I haven't really thought about it. I mean, usually, I like restoring vintage surveying equipment. I have this gorgeous 1940s brass transit level that I bought at an estate sale a while back. The craftsmanship on those old instruments is just incredible. They used to machine the brass so precisely, long before AutoCAD or CNC machines were even a concept. \n\nActually, my ex-husband knocked it off my workbench once. He was trying to reach a golf club or something in the garage. He played golf every single Sunday with his boss, which was always just an excuse to drink scotch at 10 AM, if you ask me. He had this terrible swing, too, completely off-balance. The physics of his stance were just a total disaster, no center of gravity whatsoever. But um... yeah, the transit level got a little dented when it fell. I was going to try and buff it out, but... I don't know. What did you ask me? Something about my hobbies? I just haven't really been paying attention to what I like lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in restoring vintage surveying equipment over the past month?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, I guess I have. I haven't touched the transit level in weeks. It's just sitting there on the workbench next to all my polishing cloths. I used to buy those microfiber cloths in bulk from this industrial supply catalog. They're actually meant for cleaning optical lenses on satellites, which is fascinating if you think about it. The tolerances for space-grade optics are so tight that even a microscopic speck of dust can ruin a million-dollar image. \n\nMy ex-husband thought it was ridiculous that I spent so much on cleaning cloths. He used to just wipe his glasses on his shirt, which drove me absolutely insane. You can literally see the micro-scratches forming on the anti-reflective coating when you do that. He had these horrible wire-rimmed frames, too. They made him look like a low-budget accountant from the 90s. I told him a hundred times to get thicker frames, maybe something in a tortoiseshell, to balance out his jawline, but he never listened. He was stubborn like that about everything. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? The surveying equipment? Yeah, I just... I look at it, and I know I should want to fix it, but I just don't have the energy to even pick up the polishing cloth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um... I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale. I actually threw my scale out a few months ago because it was one of those smart scales that syncs to your phone via Bluetooth, and the app was just terribly designed. The user interface was a nightmare. They used this awful neon green font on a white background, which completely violates basic accessibility standards for contrast ratios. I don't know who their software engineers were, but they clearly didn't do any beta testing with actual humans. \n\nMy ex-husband loved that scale, though. He was obsessed with tracking his BMI, even though BMI is a completely outdated metric that doesn't account for muscle mass or bone density. Not that he had much muscle mass to speak of. He tried to get into CrossFit for about three weeks, bought all the expensive gear, the specialized shoes with the flat soles, the knee sleeves... and then he pulled a hamstring doing a box jump because his form was just atrocious. I tried to explain the biomechanics of a proper landing to him, how you need to absorb the kinetic energy through your hips and knees, but he just got defensive. \n\nSo yeah, I threw the scale away after he moved out. I haven't really noticed if my clothes fit differently or anything. I just... I haven't been eating much, I guess, but I don't know about my weight.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? I... no, not really. I don't think so. I tried to read a bit the other day, I have this stack of structural engineering journals that I usually devour. There was this one article about the tensile strength of a new carbon-fiber reinforced concrete that they're using in bridge construction in Japan. It's fascinating because earthquakes over there create such unique shear forces, and the way they calculate the load distribution is completely different from how we do it here. 

My ex-husband actually wanted to go to Japan for our honeymoon, but he just wanted to eat sushi and go to those weird robot cafes. He didn't appreciate the infrastructure at all. We ended up going to Florida instead, which was a nightmare. The humidity was awful, and he insisted on staying at this resort where the foundation was clearly settling unevenly. I could see the hairline cracks radiating from the window frames in our room. I pointed it out to the manager, explained how the differential settlement was going to cause major structural issues down the line if they didn't underpin the foundation, but they just looked at me like I was crazy and offered us a free breakfast voucher. 

But um... no. I don't think there's anything I'm really enjoying right now. I just sit around mostly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Harming myself? Um... I mean, I guess I've been thinking a lot lately that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. It actually got intense enough a few nights ago that I scared myself, so I ended up calling the employee assistance program at my firm. 

Which, honestly, their hotline routing system is incredibly poorly designed. I was on hold for almost twenty minutes listening to this awful synthesized jazz, and the audio compression on the track was so aggressive that it was literally clipping the high frequencies. It sounded like a heavily degraded file from the early days of the internet. My ex-husband used to listen to music exactly like that. He refused to pay for premium streaming, so he just ripped these low-quality audio files from random videos online. The acoustic degradation was abysmal. I tried to explain the concept of lossless audio formats to him, how the bit depth and sample rate completely change the dynamic range of the waveform, but he just rolled his eyes and said I was being an elitist. He always did that when he didn't understand the math or the physics behind something. 

But um... what was I saying? Right, the EAP line. Yeah, I called them because the thoughts were just getting really overwhelming. I just keep thinking about not waking up anymore.

How? Um... well, like I was just saying, it's mostly just that overwhelming feeling that it would be so much easier if I just didn't wake up. I don't really have a... a schematic for it, or a specific plan. The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. I haven't been eating much, I guess, but I don't know about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no, not really. I don't think so. I tried to read a bit the other day, I have this stack of structural engineering journals that I usually devour. There was this one article about the tensile strength of a new carbon-fiber reinforced concrete that they're using in bridge construction in Japan. It's fascinating because earthquakes over there create such unique shear forces, and the way they calculate the load distribution is completely different from how we do it here. \n\nMy ex-husband actually wanted to go to Japan for our honeymoon, but he just wanted to eat sushi and go to those weird robot cafes. He didn't appreciate the infrastructure at all. We ended up going to Florida instead, which was a nightmare. The humidity was awful, and he insisted on staying at this resort where the foundation was clearly settling unevenly. I could see the hairline cracks radiating from the window frames in our room. I pointed it out to the manager, explained how the differential settlement was going to cause major structural issues down the line if they didn't underpin the foundation, but they just looked at me like I was crazy and offered us a free breakfast voucher. \n\nBut um... no. I don't think there's anything I'm really enjoying right now. I just sit around mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Um... I mean, I guess I've been thinking a lot lately that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. It actually got intense enough a few nights ago that I scared myself, so I ended up calling the employee assistance program at my firm. \n\nWhich, honestly, their hotline routing system is incredibly poorly designed. I was on hold for almost twenty minutes listening to this awful synthesized jazz, and the audio compression on the track was so aggressive that it was literally clipping the high frequencies. It sounded like a heavily degraded file from the early days of the internet. My ex-husband used to listen to music exactly like that. He refused to pay for premium streaming, so he just ripped these low-quality audio files from random videos online. The acoustic degradation was abysmal. I tried to explain the concept of lossless audio formats to him, how the bit depth and sample rate completely change the dynamic range of the waveform, but he just rolled his eyes and said I was being an elitist. He always did that when he didn't understand the math or the physics behind something. \n\nBut um... what was I saying? Right, the EAP line. Yeah, I called them because the thoughts were just getting really overwhelming. I just keep thinking about not waking up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you decided to act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: How? Um... well, like I was just saying, it's mostly just that overwhelming feeling that it would be so much easier if I just didn't wake up. I don't really have a... a schematic for it, or a specific plan. Which is frustrating, because my ex-husband actually took our good mattress when he moved out, so I'm stuck with this cheap memory foam one he had bought for the guest room. He ordered it from one of those trendy bed-in-a-box companies just because he liked their podcast advertisements. The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. He always dismissed basic physics if it conflicted with something he bought on the internet. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, a plan. No. No specific plan. Just what I said before, about wanting to not wake up anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any small pleasures in your daily life that you still look forward to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Small pleasures? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I guess I used to look forward to my morning coffee, but even that's just... it's just a routine now. I have this burr grinder that I bought a few years ago, it's actually a really beautiful piece of machinery. The way the conical burrs crush the beans instead of chopping them, it creates a much more uniform particle size distribution. It's crucial for achieving the right extraction yield, especially if you're doing a pour-over. 

My ex-husband hated it, of course. He said it was too loud and took too much effort. He preferred those plastic pod machines that just blast hot water through stale grounds. The pressure dynamics in those things are a joke, and the water temperature is never consistent enough for proper solubilization. I tried to show him the extraction curve, how the under-extracted compounds make it taste sour and the over-extracted ones make it bitter, but he just said coffee is coffee and I was being ridiculous. He actually bought one of those pod machines for the kitchen, and it leaked everywhere because the internal tubing was cheap PVC that couldn't handle the thermal expansion. It ruined a section of the hardwood flooring near the counter. 

But um... no. I don't really look forward to anything right now. Not even coffee. Everything just feels... flat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Um... yeah. Yeah, I feel guilty about pretty much everything. I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible engineer, a completely failed wife, just... a burden to everyone around me. I lie awake at night and just replay every single missed calculation I've ever made, or every slightly tense email I've sent, and it just feels like absolute proof that I ruin everything I touch. 

Like this one email I sent to a contractor last month about the load-bearing capacity of a steel beam we were speccing for a pedestrian bridge. I used the standard yield strength for A992 structural steel, which is 50 ksi, but I didn't explicitly account for the localized stress concentrations near the welded joints in my initial draft. It was a minor oversight, caught immediately in the secondary review, but it's just... I should have known better. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. 

My ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He'd just fire off these one-sentence replies with typos in them, completely unbothered. He actually sent an email to his boss once where he misspelled the company's name. I tried to explain to him that professional communication is an extension of your competence, that the structural integrity of a sentence is just as important as the structural integrity of a bridge, but he just laughed and said I was being rigid. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. 

But um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything. I replay all of it, the emails, the math, the marriage, and it just proves I ruin things.

I worked on the preliminary calculations for that a few years ago. My ex-husband used to absolutely hate driving under overpasses, he always swore they were going to cave in on his car. Which is ironic, I guess, because right now it feels like everything I built for myself after the divorce actually has collapsed, and I’m just... sitting under it. Like I'm trapped under all the rubble and I can't lift it. \n\nOh, by the way, did you notice the hairline crack in the drywall out in your waiting room? It’s probably just normal building settlement, but I couldn't stop staring at it while I was sitting out there. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because of that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: It's been... I'd say it's been hanging over me for about a month now. It really started getting bad right around all the financial mess after the divorce. Dividing everything up was just... well, I had to build this massive Excel spreadsheet to track all our joint assets and the legal fees. I actually color-coded the whole thing. I don't know if you use Excel much, but people really don't utilize the pivot table function nearly enough. My ex-husband definitely didn't. He couldn't even figure out how to sum a basic column without calling me into the home office to do it for him. It's funny how someone can be so helpless with basic software. But um... yeah, ever since that whole financial disaster really finalized about four weeks ago, this heavy feeling just hasn't lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I... I really couldn't tell you. I haven't really thought about it. I mean, usually, I like restoring vintage surveying equipment. I have this gorgeous 1940s brass transit level that I bought at an estate sale a while back. The craftsmanship on those old instruments is just incredible. They used to machine the brass so precisely, long before AutoCAD or CNC machines were even a concept. \n\nActually, my ex-husband knocked it off my workbench once. He was trying to reach a golf club or something in the garage. He played golf every single Sunday with his boss, which was always just an excuse to drink scotch at 10 AM, if you ask me. He had this terrible swing, too, completely off-balance. The physics of his stance were just a total disaster, no center of gravity whatsoever. But um... yeah, the transit level got a little dented when it fell. I was going to try and buff it out, but... I don't know. What did you ask me? Something about my hobbies? I just haven't really been paying attention to what I like lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in restoring vintage surveying equipment over the past month?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, I guess I have. I haven't touched the transit level in weeks. It's just sitting there on the workbench next to all my polishing cloths. I used to buy those microfiber cloths in bulk from this industrial supply catalog. They're actually meant for cleaning optical lenses on satellites, which is fascinating if you think about it. The tolerances for space-grade optics are so tight that even a microscopic speck of dust can ruin a million-dollar image. \n\nMy ex-husband thought it was ridiculous that I spent so much on cleaning cloths. He used to just wipe his glasses on his shirt, which drove me absolutely insane. You can literally see the micro-scratches forming on the anti-reflective coating when you do that. He had these horrible wire-rimmed frames, too. They made him look like a low-budget accountant from the 90s. I told him a hundred times to get thicker frames, maybe something in a tortoiseshell, to balance out his jawline, but he never listened. He was stubborn like that about everything. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? The surveying equipment? Yeah, I just... I look at it, and I know I should want to fix it, but I just don't have the energy to even pick up the polishing cloth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um... I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale. I actually threw my scale out a few months ago because it was one of those smart scales that syncs to your phone via Bluetooth, and the app was just terribly designed. The user interface was a nightmare. They used this awful neon green font on a white background, which completely violates basic accessibility standards for contrast ratios. I don't know who their software engineers were, but they clearly didn't do any beta testing with actual humans. \n\nMy ex-husband loved that scale, though. He was obsessed with tracking his BMI, even though BMI is a completely outdated metric that doesn't account for muscle mass or bone density. Not that he had much muscle mass to speak of. He tried to get into CrossFit for about three weeks, bought all the expensive gear, the specialized shoes with the flat soles, the knee sleeves... and then he pulled a hamstring doing a box jump because his form was just atrocious. I tried to explain the biomechanics of a proper landing to him, how you need to absorb the kinetic energy through your hips and knees, but he just got defensive. \n\nSo yeah, I threw the scale away after he moved out. I haven't really noticed if my clothes fit differently or anything. I just... I haven't been eating much, I guess, but I don't know about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no, not really. I don't think so. I tried to read a bit the other day, I have this stack of structural engineering journals that I usually devour. There was this one article about the tensile strength of a new carbon-fiber reinforced concrete that they're using in bridge construction in Japan. It's fascinating because earthquakes over there create such unique shear forces, and the way they calculate the load distribution is completely different from how we do it here. \n\nMy ex-husband actually wanted to go to Japan for our honeymoon, but he just wanted to eat sushi and go to those weird robot cafes. He didn't appreciate the infrastructure at all. We ended up going to Florida instead, which was a nightmare. The humidity was awful, and he insisted on staying at this resort where the foundation was clearly settling unevenly. I could see the hairline cracks radiating from the window frames in our room. I pointed it out to the manager, explained how the differential settlement was going to cause major structural issues down the line if they didn't underpin the foundation, but they just looked at me like I was crazy and offered us a free breakfast voucher. \n\nBut um... no. I don't think there's anything I'm really enjoying right now. I just sit around mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Um... I mean, I guess I've been thinking a lot lately that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. It actually got intense enough a few nights ago that I scared myself, so I ended up calling the employee assistance program at my firm. \n\nWhich, honestly, their hotline routing system is incredibly poorly designed. I was on hold for almost twenty minutes listening to this awful synthesized jazz, and the audio compression on the track was so aggressive that it was literally clipping the high frequencies. It sounded like a heavily degraded file from the early days of the internet. My ex-husband used to listen to music exactly like that. He refused to pay for premium streaming, so he just ripped these low-quality audio files from random videos online. The acoustic degradation was abysmal. I tried to explain the concept of lossless audio formats to him, how the bit depth and sample rate completely change the dynamic range of the waveform, but he just rolled his eyes and said I was being an elitist. He always did that when he didn't understand the math or the physics behind something. \n\nBut um... what was I saying? Right, the EAP line. Yeah, I called them because the thoughts were just getting really overwhelming. Which is frustrating, because my ex-husband actually took our good mattress when he moved out, so I'm stuck with this cheap memory foam one he had bought for the guest room. He ordered it from one of those trendy bed-in-a-box companies just because he liked their podcast advertisements. The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. He always dismissed basic physics if it conflicted with something he bought on the internet. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, a plan. No. No specific plan. I have this burr grinder that I bought a few years ago, it's actually a really beautiful piece of machinery. The way the conical burrs crush the beans instead of chopping them, it creates a much more uniform particle size distribution. It's crucial for achieving the right extraction yield, especially if you're doing a pour-over. \n\nMy ex-husband hated it, of course. He said it was too loud and took too much effort. He preferred those plastic pod machines that just blast hot water through stale grounds. The pressure dynamics in those things are a joke, and the water temperature is never consistent enough for proper solubilization. I tried to show him the extraction curve, how the under-extracted compounds make it taste sour and the over-extracted ones make it bitter, but he just said coffee is coffee and I was being ridiculous. He actually bought one of those pod machines for the kitchen, and it leaked everywhere because the internal tubing was cheap PVC that couldn't handle the thermal expansion. It ruined a section of the hardwood flooring near the counter. \n\nBut um... no. I don't really look forward to anything right now. Not even coffee. Everything just feels... flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... yeah. Yeah, I feel guilty about pretty much everything. I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible engineer, a completely failed wife, just... a burden to everyone around me. I lie awake at night and just replay every single missed calculation I've ever made, or every slightly tense email I've sent, and it just feels like absolute proof that I ruin everything I touch. \n\nLike this one email I sent to a contractor last month about the load-bearing capacity of a steel beam we were speccing for a pedestrian bridge. I used the standard yield strength for A992 structural steel, which is 50 ksi, but I didn't explicitly account for the localized stress concentrations near the welded joints in my initial draft. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. \n\nMy ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Activities I used to enjoy? Um... well, I used to really love gardening. I had this whole setup on the balcony of our old apartment. I mean, I guess I haven't even touched a plant since the divorce. It just feels pointless now. I had this amazing hydroponic system I built from scratch. It was a nutrient film technique setup, where a very shallow stream of water containing all the dissolved nutrients recirculates past the bare roots of the plants in a watertight channel. 

It was brilliant, honestly. The fluid dynamics involved in maintaining the exact flow rate—you need it just fast enough to deliver nutrients but slow enough to allow for optimal oxygenation at the root zone. I calculated the exact slope of the PVC channels using the Manning equation to account for the friction of the pipe material. 

My ex-husband complained about the sound of the water pump. He said it sounded like a leaky toilet. He actually unplugged it once because he was trying to watch some action movie and said the trickling was distracting. He completely crashed the dissolved oxygen levels in the reservoir. The roots started suffocating within hours because the boundary layer of oxygen around them depleted. I tried to explain the biochemistry of root respiration to him, how without oxygen, the plants switch to anaerobic metabolism and start producing ethanol, which basically poisons them from the inside out. But he just said they were "just tomatoes" and I could buy more at the store. He never understood the effort it takes to build a sustainable system. 

But um... sorry, what was the question? Activities I don't enjoy anymore. Yeah, gardening, I guess. I just... I don't have the energy to care about keeping anything alive right now. Not even plants. Everything is just too much effort.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can't seem to shake this really heavy, hopeless feeling lately. It's sort of like... well, I’m a civil engineer, so I think about structural integrity and load-bearing a lot. Actually, the new overpass on I-95 they just finished? I worked on the preliminary calculations for that a few years ago. My ex-husband used to absolutely hate driving under overpasses, he always swore they were going to cave in on his car. Which is ironic, I guess, because right now it feels like everything I built for myself after the divorce actually has collapsed, and I’m just... sitting under it. Like I'm trapped under all the rubble and I can't lift it. \n\nOh, by the way, did you notice the hairline crack in the drywall out in your waiting room? It’s probably just normal building settlement, but I couldn't stop staring at it while I was sitting out there. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because of that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: It's been... I'd say it's been hanging over me for about a month now. It really started getting bad right around all the financial mess after the divorce. Dividing everything up was just... well, I had to build this massive Excel spreadsheet to track all our joint assets and the legal fees. I actually color-coded the whole thing. I don't know if you use Excel much, but people really don't utilize the pivot table function nearly enough. My ex-husband definitely didn't. He couldn't even figure out how to sum a basic column without calling me into the home office to do it for him. It's funny how someone can be so helpless with basic software. But um... yeah, ever since that whole financial disaster really finalized about four weeks ago, this heavy feeling just hasn't lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I... I really couldn't tell you. I haven't really thought about it. I mean, usually, I like restoring vintage surveying equipment. I have this gorgeous 1940s brass transit level that I bought at an estate sale a while back. The craftsmanship on those old instruments is just incredible. They used to machine the brass so precisely, long before AutoCAD or CNC machines were even a concept. \n\nActually, my ex-husband knocked it off my workbench once. He was trying to reach a golf club or something in the garage. He played golf every single Sunday with his boss, which was always just an excuse to drink scotch at 10 AM, if you ask me. He had this terrible swing, too, completely off-balance. The physics of his stance were just a total disaster, no center of gravity whatsoever. But um... yeah, the transit level got a little dented when it fell. I was going to try and buff it out, but... I don't know. What did you ask me? Something about my hobbies? I just haven't really been paying attention to what I like lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in restoring vintage surveying equipment over the past month?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, I guess I have. I haven't touched the transit level in weeks. It's just sitting there on the workbench next to all my polishing cloths. I used to buy those microfiber cloths in bulk from this industrial supply catalog. They're actually meant for cleaning optical lenses on satellites, which is fascinating if you think about it. The tolerances for space-grade optics are so tight that even a microscopic speck of dust can ruin a million-dollar image. \n\nMy ex-husband thought it was ridiculous that I spent so much on cleaning cloths. He used to just wipe his glasses on his shirt, which drove me absolutely insane. You can literally see the micro-scratches forming on the anti-reflective coating when you do that. He had these horrible wire-rimmed frames, too. They made him look like a low-budget accountant from the 90s. I told him a hundred times to get thicker frames, maybe something in a tortoiseshell, to balance out his jawline, but he never listened. He was stubborn like that about everything. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? The surveying equipment? Yeah, I just... I look at it, and I know I should want to fix it, but I just don't have the energy to even pick up the polishing cloth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um... I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale. I actually threw my scale out a few months ago because it was one of those smart scales that syncs to your phone via Bluetooth, and the app was just terribly designed. The user interface was a nightmare. They used this awful neon green font on a white background, which completely violates basic accessibility standards for contrast ratios. I don't know who their software engineers were, but they clearly didn't do any beta testing with actual humans. \n\nMy ex-husband loved that scale, though. He was obsessed with tracking his BMI, even though BMI is a completely outdated metric that doesn't account for muscle mass or bone density. Not that he had much muscle mass to speak of. He tried to get into CrossFit for about three weeks, bought all the expensive gear, the specialized shoes with the flat soles, the knee sleeves... and then he pulled a hamstring doing a box jump because his form was just atrocious. I tried to explain the biomechanics of a proper landing to him, how you need to absorb the kinetic energy through your hips and knees, but he just got defensive. \n\nSo yeah, I threw the scale away after he moved out. I haven't really noticed if my clothes fit differently or anything. I just... I haven't been eating much, I guess, but I don't know about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... no, not really. I don't think so. I tried to read a bit the other day, I have this stack of structural engineering journals that I usually devour. There was this one article about the tensile strength of a new carbon-fiber reinforced concrete that they're using in bridge construction in Japan. It's fascinating because earthquakes over there create such unique shear forces, and the way they calculate the load distribution is completely different from how we do it here. \n\nMy ex-husband actually wanted to go to Japan for our honeymoon, but he just wanted to eat sushi and go to those weird robot cafes. He didn't appreciate the infrastructure at all. We ended up going to Florida instead, which was a nightmare. The humidity was awful, and he insisted on staying at this resort where the foundation was clearly settling unevenly. I could see the hairline cracks radiating from the window frames in our room. I pointed it out to the manager, explained how the differential settlement was going to cause major structural issues down the line if they didn't underpin the foundation, but they just looked at me like I was crazy and offered us a free breakfast voucher. \n\nBut um... no. I don't think there's anything I'm really enjoying right now. I just sit around mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Um... I mean, I guess I've been thinking a lot lately that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up. It actually got intense enough a few nights ago that I scared myself, so I ended up calling the employee assistance program at my firm. \n\nWhich, honestly, their hotline routing system is incredibly poorly designed. I was on hold for almost twenty minutes listening to this awful synthesized jazz, and the audio compression on the track was so aggressive that it was literally clipping the high frequencies. It sounded like a heavily degraded file from the early days of the internet. My ex-husband used to listen to music exactly like that. He refused to pay for premium streaming, so he just ripped these low-quality audio files from random videos online. The acoustic degradation was abysmal. I tried to explain the concept of lossless audio formats to him, how the bit depth and sample rate completely change the dynamic range of the waveform, but he just rolled his eyes and said I was being an elitist. He always did that when he didn't understand the math or the physics behind something. \n\nBut um... what was I saying? Right, the EAP line. Yeah, I called them because the thoughts were just getting really overwhelming. I just keep thinking about not waking up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you decided to act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: How? Um... well, like I was just saying, it's mostly just that overwhelming feeling that it would be so much easier if I just didn't wake up. I don't really have a... a schematic for it, or a specific plan. It's just that the thoughts of fading out got so intense the other night that I actually scared myself, which is why I ended up calling that EAP hotline I mentioned. \n\nI really just want to go to sleep and stay asleep. Which is frustrating, because my ex-husband actually took our good mattress when he moved out, so I'm stuck with this cheap memory foam one he had bought for the guest room. He ordered it from one of those trendy bed-in-a-box companies just because he liked their podcast advertisements. The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. He always dismissed basic physics if it conflicted with something he bought on the internet. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, a plan. No. No specific plan. Just what I said before, about wanting to not wake up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in your daily life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I guess I used to look forward to my morning coffee, but even that's just... it's just a routine now. I have this burr grinder that I bought a few years ago, it's actually a really beautiful piece of machinery. The way the conical burrs crush the beans instead of chopping them, it creates a much more uniform particle size distribution. It's crucial for achieving the right extraction yield, especially if you're doing a pour-over. \n\nMy ex-husband hated it, of course. He said it was too loud and took too much effort. He preferred those plastic pod machines that just blast hot water through stale grounds. The pressure dynamics in those things are a joke, and the water temperature is never consistent enough for proper solubilization. I tried to show him the extraction curve, how the under-extracted compounds make it taste sour and the over-extracted ones make it bitter, but he just said coffee is coffee and I was being ridiculous. He actually bought one of those pod machines for the kitchen, and it leaked everywhere because the internal tubing was cheap PVC that couldn't handle the thermal expansion. It ruined a section of the hardwood flooring near the counter. \n\nBut um... no. I don't really look forward to anything right now. Not even coffee. Everything just feels... flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... yeah. Yeah, I feel guilty about pretty much everything. I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible engineer, a completely failed wife, just... a burden to everyone around me. I lie awake at night and just replay every single missed calculation I've ever made, or every slightly tense email I've sent, and it just feels like absolute proof that I ruin everything I touch. \n\nLike this one email I sent to a contractor last month about the load-bearing capacity of a steel beam we were speccing for a pedestrian bridge. I used the standard yield strength for A992 structural steel, which is 50 ksi, but I didn't explicitly account for the localized stress concentrations near the welded joints in my initial draft. It was a minor oversight, caught immediately in the secondary review, but it's just... I should have known better. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. \n\nMy ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He'd just fire off these one-sentence replies with typos in them, completely unbothered. He actually sent an email to his boss once where he misspelled the company's name. I tried to explain to him that professional communication is an extension of your competence, that the structural integrity of a sentence is just as important as the structural integrity of a bridge, but he just laughed and said I was being rigid. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything. I replay all of it, the emails, the math, the marriage, and it just proves I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive pleasure from?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Um... well, I used to really love gardening. I had this whole setup on the balcony of our old apartment. I mean, I guess I haven't even touched a plant since the divorce. It just feels pointless now. I had this amazing hydroponic system I built from scratch. It was a nutrient film technique setup, where a very shallow stream of water containing all the dissolved nutrients recirculates past the bare roots of the plants in a watertight channel. \n\nIt was brilliant, honestly. The fluid dynamics involved in maintaining the exact flow rate—you need it just fast enough to deliver nutrients but slow enough to allow for optimal oxygenation at the root zone. I calculated the exact slope of the PVC channels using the Manning equation to account for the friction of the pipe material. \n\nMy ex-husband complained about the sound of the water pump. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? Um... yeah, it's been pretty bad. Lately I either lie awake staring at the ceiling replaying bills and work mistakes, or I wake up before dawn feeling like I never actually slept at all. It's just this constant state of exhaustion. 

I actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. I bought these blackout curtains and installed them using heavy-duty anchors because the drywall in my apartment is terrible. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. Otherwise, the cyclic loading just pulverizes the gypsum core and the screws pull right out. 

My ex-husband never understood that. He tried to hang a massive, heavy mirror in our hallway once using just regular plastic expansion anchors. I told him the shear stress was going to be too much for the drywall without finding a stud, but he just grabbed his drill and said I was overthinking it. Two days later, the whole thing ripped out of the wall and shattered everywhere. It gouged the drywall so badly I had to cut out a whole square, patch it with a new piece of gypsum board, tape the seams, and apply three coats of joint compound, sanding between each one to get a smooth finish. He didn't even help clean up the glass. He just said "well, it looked fine when I hung it" and went back to watching TV. 

But um... what were we talking about? Oh, sleep. Yeah. I just... I can't sleep. I just lie there and think about everything I've messed up, or I wake up at 4 AM and just feel... empty.

The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. He always dismissed basic physics if it conflicted with something he bought on the internet. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, a plan. No. No specific plan. Just what I said before, about wanting to not wake up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in your daily life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I guess I used to look forward to my morning coffee, but even that's just... it's just a routine now. I have this burr grinder that I bought a few years ago, it's actually a really beautiful piece of machinery. The way the conical burrs crush the beans instead of chopping them, it creates a much more uniform particle size distribution. It's crucial for achieving the right extraction yield, especially if you're doing a pour-over. \n\nMy ex-husband hated it, of course. He said it was too loud and took too much effort. He preferred those plastic pod machines that just blast hot water through stale grounds. The pressure dynamics in those things are a joke, and the water temperature is never consistent enough for proper solubilization. I tried to show him the extraction curve, how the under-extracted compounds make it taste sour and the over-extracted ones make it bitter, but he just said coffee is coffee and I was being ridiculous. He actually bought one of those pod machines for the kitchen, and it leaked everywhere because the internal tubing was cheap PVC that couldn't handle the thermal expansion. It ruined a section of the hardwood flooring near the counter. \n\nBut um... no. I don't really look forward to anything right now. Not even coffee. Everything just feels... flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... yeah. Yeah, I feel guilty about pretty much everything. I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible engineer, a completely failed wife, just... a burden to everyone around me. I lie awake at night and just replay every single missed calculation I've ever made, or every slightly tense email I've sent, and it just feels like absolute proof that I ruin everything I touch. \n\nLike this one email I sent to a contractor last month about the load-bearing capacity of a steel beam we were speccing for a pedestrian bridge. I used the standard yield strength for A992 structural steel, which is 50 ksi, but I didn't explicitly account for the localized stress concentrations near the welded joints in my initial draft. It was a minor oversight, caught immediately in the secondary review, but it's just... I should have known better. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. \n\nMy ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He'd just fire off these one-sentence replies with typos in them, completely unbothered. He actually sent an email to his boss once where he misspelled the company's name. I tried to explain to him that professional communication is an extension of your competence, that the structural integrity of a sentence is just as important as the structural integrity of a bridge, but he just laughed and said I was being rigid. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything. I replay all of it, the emails, the math, the marriage, and it just proves I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive pleasure from?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Um... well, I used to really love gardening. I had this whole setup on the balcony of our old apartment. I mean, I guess I haven't even touched a plant since the divorce. It just feels pointless now. I had this amazing hydroponic system I built from scratch. It was a nutrient film technique setup, where a very shallow stream of water containing all the dissolved nutrients recirculates past the bare roots of the plants in a watertight channel. \n\nIt was brilliant, honestly. The fluid dynamics involved in maintaining the exact flow rate—you need it just fast enough to deliver nutrients but slow enough to allow for optimal oxygenation at the root zone. He completely crashed the dissolved oxygen levels in the reservoir. The roots started suffocating within hours because the boundary layer of oxygen around them depleted. I tried to explain the biochemistry of root respiration to him, how without oxygen, the plants switch to anaerobic metabolism and start producing ethanol, which basically poisons them from the inside out. But he just said they were \"just tomatoes\" and I could buy more at the store. He never understood the effort it takes to build a sustainable system. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was the question? Activities I don't enjoy anymore. Yeah, gardening, I guess. I just... I don't have the energy to care about keeping anything alive right now. Not even plants. Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your sleep patterns over the past month?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... yeah, it's been pretty bad. Lately I either lie awake staring at the ceiling replaying bills and work mistakes, or I wake up before dawn feeling like I never actually slept at all. It's just this constant state of exhaustion. \n\nI actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. It was a minor oversight, caught immediately in the secondary review, but it's just... I should have known better. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. \n\nMy ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He'd just fire off these one-sentence replies with typos in them, completely unbothered. He actually sent an email to his boss once where he misspelled the company's name. I tried to explain to him that professional communication is an extension of your competence, that the structural integrity of a sentence is just as important as the structural integrity of a bridge, but he just laughed and said I was being rigid. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything. I replay all of it, the emails, the math, the marriage, and it just proves I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive pleasure from?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Um... well, I used to really love gardening. I had this whole setup on the balcony of our old apartment. I mean, I guess I haven't even touched a plant since the divorce. It just feels pointless now. I had this amazing hydroponic system I built from scratch. It was a nutrient film technique setup, where a very shallow stream of water containing all the dissolved nutrients recirculates past the bare roots of the plants in a watertight channel. \n\nIt was brilliant, honestly. The fluid dynamics involved in maintaining the exact flow rate—you need it just fast enough to deliver nutrients but slow enough to allow for optimal oxygenation at the root zone. I calculated the exact slope of the PVC channels using the Manning equation to account for the friction of the pipe material. \n\nMy ex-husband complained about the sound of the water pump. He said it sounded like a leaky toilet. He actually unplugged it once because he was trying to watch some action movie and said the trickling was distracting. He completely crashed the dissolved oxygen levels in the reservoir. The roots started suffocating within hours because the boundary layer of oxygen around them depleted. I tried to explain the biochemistry of root respiration to him, how without oxygen, the plants switch to anaerobic metabolism and start producing ethanol, which basically poisons them from the inside out. But he just said they were \"just tomatoes\" and I could buy more at the store. He never understood the effort it takes to build a sustainable system. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was the question? Activities I don't enjoy anymore. Yeah, gardening, I guess. I just... I don't have the energy to care about keeping anything alive right now. Not even plants. Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your sleep patterns over the past month?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... yeah, it's been pretty bad. Lately I either lie awake staring at the ceiling replaying bills and work mistakes, or I wake up before dawn feeling like I never actually slept at all. It's just this constant state of exhaustion. \n\nI actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. I bought these blackout curtains and installed them using heavy-duty anchors because the drywall in my apartment is terrible. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. Otherwise, the cyclic loading just pulverizes the gypsum core and the screws pull right out. \n\nMy ex-husband never understood that. He tried to hang a massive, heavy mirror in our hallway once using just regular plastic expansion anchors. I told him the shear stress was going to be too much for the drywall without finding a stud, but he just grabbed his drill and said I was overthinking it. Two days later, the whole thing ripped out of the wall and shattered everywhere. It gouged the drywall so badly I had to cut out a whole square, patch it with a new piece of gypsum board, tape the seams, and apply three coats of joint compound, sanding between each one to get a smooth finish. He didn't even help clean up the glass. He just said \"well, it looked fine when I hung it\" and went back to watching TV. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, sleep. Yeah. I just... I can't sleep. I just lie there and think about everything I've messed up, or I wake up at 4 AM and just feel... empty.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I've tried to think of something, but nothing really feels good anymore. Everything just feels like work. Even eating is just... mechanical. 

I did try to make some pasta the other night, just to have something warm. I was boiling the water, and I started thinking about the thermodynamics of the phase change, you know? The latent heat of vaporization for water is about 2260 kilojoules per kilogram, which is incredibly high compared to other common liquids because of the extensive hydrogen bonding between the water molecules. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. 

My ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. He'd always turn the burner up to the absolute maximum, even if he was just heating up a small pot for ramen. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. He actually melted the handle off my favorite saucepan once because the flame was too wide for the base of the pot, and the convective heat transfer just completely warped the phenolic resin of the handle. 

But um... sorry. What did you ask me? Oh, right, pleasure. No, there's nothing. Even the things that used to distract me just feel heavy now. I don't enjoy anything.

I can't sleep. I just lie there and think about everything I've messed up, or I wake up at 4 AM and just feel... empty.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I've tried to think of something, but nothing really feels good anymore. Everything just feels like work. Even eating is just... mechanical. \n\nI did try to make some pasta the other night, just to have something warm. I was boiling the water, and I started thinking about the thermodynamics of the phase change, you know? The latent heat of vaporization for water is about 2260 kilojoules per kilogram, which is incredibly high compared to other common liquids because of the extensive hydrogen bonding between the water molecules. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. \n\nMy ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. He'd always turn the burner up to the absolute maximum, even if he was just heating up a small pot for ramen. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. He actually melted the handle off my favorite saucepan once because the flame was too wide for the base of the pot, and the convective heat transfer just completely warped the phenolic resin of the handle. \n\nBut um... sorry. What did you ask me? Oh, right, pleasure. No, there's nothing. Even the things that used to distract me just feel heavy now. I don't enjoy anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. \n\nMy ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. He actually melted the handle off my favorite saucepan once because the flame was too wide for the base of the pot, and the convective heat transfer just completely warped the phenolic resin of the handle. \n\nBut um... sorry. What did you ask me? Oh, right, pleasure. No, there's nothing. Even the things that used to distract me just feel heavy now. I don't enjoy anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities that you once found enjoyable for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past month?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Loss of interest? Um... yeah, I guess so. Every day. It's just... I wake up and I just don't care about anything. I don't want to go to work, I don't want to see anyone. It's all just... blank.

I used to really love drafting, you know? Just sitting down with AutoCAD and designing structural supports. I used to get so absorbed in calculating the load-bearing capacities of different steel beams. The yield strength of A36 structural steel is about 36,000 pounds per square inch, and I'd spend hours optimizing the beam placement to ensure the maximum bending moment never exceeded the allowable stress, factoring in a safety margin of course. It's a delicate balance of material efficiency and structural integrity. 

My ex-husband used to complain that I brought my work home too much. He said I was obsessed with numbers and couldn't just relax. We were at a restaurant once, and I was looking at the exposed ceiling, trying to estimate the dead load of the HVAC system hanging from the bar joists. I pointed out that the bridging between the joists looked insufficient to prevent lateral torsional buckling under a full snow load on the roof. He got so mad. He told me to stop analyzing the building and just eat my salad. He said I ruined every date night by turning it into a site inspection. He never appreciated how beautiful it is when a structure is perfectly balanced, how all the forces resolve to zero. He just wanted to drink his beer and talk about his fantasy football team. 

But um... what were we talking about? Loss of interest? Yeah. I just... I don't even open AutoCAD anymore. I haven't looked at a blueprint in weeks. I just stare at my computer screen and nothing happens. I just don't have it in me anymore.

Worse in the morning? Um... I don't know, it's just... it's just always there. The whole day is just a flat line. I guess waking up is the hardest part because you have to actually face the fact that you have to get through another day of feeling like this. 

I remember when mornings used to mean something. I used to get up at 5:30 AM and go for a run, and I'd watch the sun come up over the suspension bridge downtown. It's a really fascinating structure, actually. It uses a continuous truss system, which is incredibly efficient for distributing live loads across multiple spans. The tension cables are anchored deep into the bedrock, and the way they transfer the compressive forces down the main pylons is just a beautiful example of statics. You have to account for thermal expansion, too, especially with the temperature differentials we get here. The expansion joints on the deck can accommodate up to eight inches of movement. 

My ex-husband hated that bridge. He said the tolls were a rip-off and he'd always take the long way around just to save two dollars, even if it cost him more in gas and time. I tried to calculate the fuel consumption of his truck versus the toll cost once, just to show him the math, but he wouldn't listen. He said I was always trying to prove I was smarter than him. He actually got a speeding ticket once on the detour route because he was so mad about the bridge, and then he blamed me for it because I had suggested we just pay the toll. He refused to pay the ticket for months until his license got suspended. 

But um... sorry. The morning. Right. I don't really notice a difference between morning or afternoon anymore. It's all just... heavy. I just don't care about any of it.

Moving slowly? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't feel restless either. I just... I mostly just sit on the couch. 

I was sitting there yesterday, actually, just staring at the coffee table. It's a glass top with a wrought iron base, and I started thinking about the modulus of elasticity of the glass. It's an amorphous solid, so it doesn't have a crystalline structure, which means its fracture mechanics are completely different from metals. When glass breaks, the crack propagation can happen at thousands of meters per second because there are no grain boundaries to arrest the crack tip. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. 

My ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. Well, the original glass top, anyway. He was watching a football game and his team threw an interception, and he just slammed his beer mug down on the table as hard as he could. He didn't even use a coaster. The impact stress exceeded the ultimate tensile strength of the glass and it just shattered everywhere. I tried to explain to him that the localized stress concentration from the heavy base of the mug caused a catastrophic failure, but he just yelled at me for caring more about the table than his team losing. He made me clean up all the glass while he went to the bedroom to sulk. I had to custom order a replacement piece of tempered glass with a beveled edge, and he complained about how much it cost for weeks. 

But um... what did you ask? Moving slowly? No. I just... I don't really move much at all. There's just no point.
