Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your sleep patterns over the past month?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... yeah, it's been pretty bad. Lately I either lie awake staring at the ceiling replaying bills and work mistakes, or I wake up before dawn feeling like I never actually slept at all. It's just this constant state of exhaustion. \n\nI actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. I bought these blackout curtains and installed them using heavy-duty anchors because the drywall in my apartment is terrible. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. Otherwise, the cyclic loading just pulverizes the gypsum core and the screws pull right out. \n\nMy ex-husband never understood that. He tried to hang a massive, heavy mirror in our hallway once using just regular plastic expansion anchors. I told him the shear stress was going to be too much for the drywall without finding a stud, but he just grabbed his drill and said I was overthinking it. Two days later, the whole thing ripped out of the wall and shattered everywhere. It gouged the drywall so badly I had to cut out a whole square, patch it with a new piece of gypsum board, tape the seams, and apply three coats of joint compound, sanding between each one to get a smooth finish. He didn't even help clean up the glass. He just said \"well, it looked fine when I hung it\" and went back to watching TV. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, sleep. Yeah. I just... I can't sleep. I just lie there and think about everything I've messed up, or I wake up at 4 AM and just feel... empty.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I've tried to think of something, but nothing really feels good anymore. Everything just feels like work. Even eating is just... mechanical. \n\nI did try to make some pasta the other night, just to have something warm. I was boiling the water, and I started thinking about the thermodynamics of the phase change, you know? The latent heat of vaporization for water is about 2260 kilojoules per kilogram, which is incredibly high compared to other common liquids because of the extensive hydrogen bonding between the water molecules. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. \n\nMy ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. He'd always turn the burner up to the absolute maximum, even if he was just heating up a small pot for ramen. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. He actually melted the handle off my favorite saucepan once because the flame was too wide for the base of the pot, and the convective heat transfer just completely warped the phenolic resin of the handle. \n\nBut um... sorry. What did you ask me? Oh, right, pleasure. No, there's nothing. Even the things that used to distract me just feel heavy now. I don't enjoy anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities that you once found enjoyable for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past month?\n\nPatient: Loss of interest? Um... yeah, I guess so. Every day. It's just... I wake up and I just don't care about anything. I don't want to go to work, I don't want to see anyone. It's all just... blank.\n\nI used to really love drafting, you know? Just sitting down with AutoCAD and designing structural supports. I used to get so absorbed in calculating the load-bearing capacities of different steel beams. The yield strength of A36 structural steel is about 36,000 pounds per square inch, and I'd spend hours optimizing the beam placement to ensure the maximum bending moment never exceeded the allowable stress, factoring in a safety margin of course. It's a delicate balance of material efficiency and structural integrity. \n\nMy ex-husband used to complain that I brought my work home too much. He said I was obsessed with numbers and couldn't just relax. We were at a restaurant once, and I was looking at the exposed ceiling, trying to estimate the dead load of the HVAC system hanging from the bar joists. I pointed out that the bridging between the joists looked insufficient to prevent lateral torsional buckling under a full snow load on the roof. He got so mad. He told me to stop analyzing the building and just eat my salad. He said I ruined every date night by turning it into a site inspection. He never appreciated how beautiful it is when a structure is perfectly balanced, how all the forces resolve to zero. He just wanted to drink his beer and talk about his fantasy football team. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Loss of interest? Yeah. I just... I don't even open AutoCAD anymore. I haven't looked at a blueprint in weeks. I just stare at my computer screen and nothing happens. I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is worse in the morning or does it stay fairly consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Worse in the morning? Um... I don't know, it's just... it's just always there. The whole day is just a flat line. I guess waking up is the hardest part because you have to actually face the fact that you have to get through another day of feeling like this. \n\nI remember when mornings used to mean something. I used to get up at 5:30 AM and go for a run, and I'd watch the sun come up over the suspension bridge downtown. It's a really fascinating structure, actually. It uses a continuous truss system, which is incredibly efficient for distributing live loads across multiple spans. The tension cables are anchored deep into the bedrock, and the way they transfer the compressive forces down the main pylons is just a beautiful example of statics. You have to account for thermal expansion, too, especially with the temperature differentials we get here. The expansion joints on the deck can accommodate up to eight inches of movement. \n\nMy ex-husband hated that bridge. He said the tolls were a rip-off and he'd always take the long way around just to save two dollars, even if it cost him more in gas and time. I tried to calculate the fuel consumption of his truck versus the toll cost once, just to show him the math, but he wouldn't listen. He said I was always trying to prove I was smarter than him. He actually got a speeding ticket once on the detour route because he was so mad about the bridge, and then he blamed me for it because I had suggested we just pay the toll. He refused to pay the ticket for months until his license got suspended. \n\nBut um... sorry. The morning. Right. I don't really notice a difference between morning or afternoon anymore. It's all just... heavy. I just don't care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're stuck in place, or the opposite, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't feel restless either. I just... I mostly just sit on the couch. \n\nI was sitting there yesterday, actually, just staring at the coffee table. It's a glass top with a wrought iron base, and I started thinking about the modulus of elasticity of the glass. It's an amorphous solid, so it doesn't have a crystalline structure, which means its fracture mechanics are completely different from metals. When glass breaks, the crack propagation can happen at thousands of meters per second because there are no grain boundaries to arrest the crack tip. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. \n\nMy ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. Well, the original glass top, anyway. He was watching a football game and his team threw an interception, and he just slammed his beer mug down on the table as hard as he could. He didn't even use a coaster. The impact stress exceeded the ultimate tensile strength of the glass and it just shattered everywhere. I tried to explain to him that the localized stress concentration from the heavy base of the mug caused a catastrophic failure, but he just yelled at me for caring more about the table than his team losing. He made me clean up all the glass while he went to the bedroom to sulk. I had to custom order a replacement piece of tempered glass with a beveled edge, and he complained about how much it cost for weeks. \n\nBut um... what did you ask? Moving slowly? No. I just... I don't really move much at all. There's just no point.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's an amorphous solid, so it doesn't have a crystalline structure, which means its fracture mechanics are completely different from metals. When glass breaks, the crack propagation can happen at thousands of meters per second because there are no grain boundaries to arrest the crack tip. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. \n\nMy ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. He didn't even use a coaster. The impact stress exceeded the ultimate tensile strength of the glass and it just shattered everywhere. I tried to explain to him that the localized stress concentration from the heavy base of the mug caused a catastrophic failure, but he just yelled at me for caring more about the table than his team losing. He made me clean up all the glass while he went to the bedroom to sulk. I had to custom order a replacement piece of tempered glass with a beveled edge, and he complained about how much it cost for weeks. \n\nBut um... what did you ask? Moving slowly? No. I just... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... didn't you just ask me that? Loss of interest? Yeah, like I said, I just... I don't care about anything anymore. The drafting, the blueprints, all of it. 

I used to really enjoy going to the botanical gardens on the weekends. They have this incredible geodesic dome greenhouse. The structural efficiency of a geodesic dome is amazing. It encloses the maximum volume of space with the minimum amount of surface area, and the spherical shape distributes stress perfectly along the triangular framework. Buckminster Fuller really popularized the design, but the actual math behind the structural load distribution is fascinating. The wind load just flows right around it, and the snow load is minimal because of the curvature. 

My ex-husband went with me once, and he hated it. It was incredibly humid inside the dome to support the tropical plants, and he just complained the whole time about sweating through his shirt. I was trying to look at the joint connections where the struts meet at the hubs—they have to be precision-engineered to handle both tension and compression depending on the external forces—and he just kept sighing loudly and asking when we could leave to get burgers. He said looking at plants was like watching paint dry. He completely missed the architectural brilliance of the structure holding the plants. 

But um... yeah. I don't go to the gardens anymore. I don't really do anything that used to bring me pleasure. It's just all... gone.

It sounded like a heavily degraded file from the early days of the internet. My ex-husband used to listen to music exactly like that. He refused to pay for premium streaming, so he just ripped these low-quality audio files from random videos online. The acoustic degradation was abysmal. I tried to explain the concept of lossless audio formats to him, how the bit depth and sample rate completely change the dynamic range of the waveform, but he just rolled his eyes and said I was being an elitist. He always did that when he didn't understand the math or the physics behind something. \n\nBut um... what was I saying? Right, the EAP line. Yeah, I called them because the thoughts were just getting really overwhelming. I just keep thinking about not waking up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you decided to act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: How? Um... well, like I was just saying, it's mostly just that overwhelming feeling that it would be so much easier if I just didn't wake up. I don't really have a... a schematic for it, or a specific plan. It's just that the thoughts of fading out got so intense the other night that I actually scared myself, which is why I ended up calling that EAP hotline I mentioned. \n\nI really just want to go to sleep and stay asleep. Which is frustrating, because my ex-husband actually took our good mattress when he moved out, so I'm stuck with this cheap memory foam one he had bought for the guest room. He ordered it from one of those trendy bed-in-a-box companies just because he liked their podcast advertisements. The viscoelastic polyurethane foam they use in those things is a complete disaster for thermal regulation. The cellular structure traps heat, so the thermal conductivity is practically zero. I tried to explain the thermodynamics to him, how the material's specific heat capacity means it basically acts as a thermal insulator and prevents your core body temperature from dropping the way it's supposed to, but he just told me I was overthinking a piece of foam. He always dismissed basic physics if it conflicted with something he bought on the internet. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, a plan. No. No specific plan. Just what I said before, about wanting to not wake up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in your daily life that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I guess I used to look forward to my morning coffee, but even that's just... it's just a routine now. I have this burr grinder that I bought a few years ago, it's actually a really beautiful piece of machinery. The way the conical burrs crush the beans instead of chopping them, it creates a much more uniform particle size distribution. It's crucial for achieving the right extraction yield, especially if you're doing a pour-over. \n\nMy ex-husband hated it, of course. He said it was too loud and took too much effort. He preferred those plastic pod machines that just blast hot water through stale grounds. The pressure dynamics in those things are a joke, and the water temperature is never consistent enough for proper solubilization. I tried to show him the extraction curve, how the under-extracted compounds make it taste sour and the over-extracted ones make it bitter, but he just said coffee is coffee and I was being ridiculous. He actually bought one of those pod machines for the kitchen, and it leaked everywhere because the internal tubing was cheap PVC that couldn't handle the thermal expansion. Everything just feels... flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... yeah. Yeah, I feel guilty about pretty much everything. I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible engineer, a completely failed wife, just... a burden to everyone around me. I lie awake at night and just replay every single missed calculation I've ever made, or every slightly tense email I've sent, and it just feels like absolute proof that I ruin everything I touch. \n\nLike this one email I sent to a contractor last month about the load-bearing capacity of a steel beam we were speccing for a pedestrian bridge. I used the standard yield strength for A992 structural steel, which is 50 ksi, but I didn't explicitly account for the localized stress concentrations near the welded joints in my initial draft. It was a minor oversight, caught immediately in the secondary review, but it's just... I should have known better. The modulus of elasticity is constant, but the stress distribution completely changes when you introduce a weld, because the heat-affected zone alters the microstructure of the steel. \n\nMy ex-husband used to say I obsessed over my emails too much. He'd just fire off these one-sentence replies with typos in them, completely unbothered. He actually sent an email to his boss once where he misspelled the company's name. I tried to explain to him that professional communication is an extension of your competence, that the structural integrity of a sentence is just as important as the structural integrity of a bridge, but he just laughed and said I was being rigid. He always called me rigid when I cared about precision. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Right, guilt. Yeah. I just... I feel like I'm a failure at everything. I replay all of it, the emails, the math, the marriage, and it just proves I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive pleasure from?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Um... well, I used to really love gardening. I had this whole setup on the balcony of our old apartment. I mean, I guess I haven't even touched a plant since the divorce. It just feels pointless now. I had this amazing hydroponic system I built from scratch. It was a nutrient film technique setup, where a very shallow stream of water containing all the dissolved nutrients recirculates past the bare roots of the plants in a watertight channel. \n\nIt was brilliant, honestly. The fluid dynamics involved in maintaining the exact flow rate—you need it just fast enough to deliver nutrients but slow enough to allow for optimal oxygenation at the root zone. I calculated the exact slope of the PVC channels using the Manning equation to account for the friction of the pipe material. \n\nMy ex-husband complained about the sound of the water pump. He said it sounded like a leaky toilet. He actually unplugged it once because he was trying to watch some action movie and said the trickling was distracting. He completely crashed the dissolved oxygen levels in the reservoir. The roots started suffocating within hours because the boundary layer of oxygen around them depleted. I tried to explain the biochemistry of root respiration to him, how without oxygen, the plants switch to anaerobic metabolism and start producing ethanol, which basically poisons them from the inside out. But he just said they were \"just tomatoes\" and I could buy more at the store. He never understood the effort it takes to build a sustainable system. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was the question? It's just this constant state of exhaustion. \n\nI actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. I bought these blackout curtains and installed them using heavy-duty anchors because the drywall in my apartment is terrible. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. Otherwise, the cyclic loading just pulverizes the gypsum core and the screws pull right out. \n\nMy ex-husband never understood that. He tried to hang a massive, heavy mirror in our hallway once using just regular plastic expansion anchors. I told him the shear stress was going to be too much for the drywall without finding a stud, but he just grabbed his drill and said I was overthinking it. Two days later, the whole thing ripped out of the wall and shattered everywhere. It gouged the drywall so badly I had to cut out a whole square, patch it with a new piece of gypsum board, tape the seams, and apply three coats of joint compound, sanding between each one to get a smooth finish. He didn't even help clean up the glass. He just said \"well, it looked fine when I hung it\" and went back to watching TV. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, sleep. Yeah. I just... Um... no. Not really. I mean, I've tried to think of something, but nothing really feels good anymore. Everything just feels like work. Even eating is just... mechanical. \n\nI did try to make some pasta the other night, just to have something warm. I was boiling the water, and I started thinking about the thermodynamics of the phase change, you know? The latent heat of vaporization for water is about 2260 kilojoules per kilogram, which is incredibly high compared to other common liquids because of the extensive hydrogen bonding between the water molecules. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. \n\nMy ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. He'd always turn the burner up to the absolute maximum, even if he was just heating up a small pot for ramen. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. I used to get so absorbed in calculating the load-bearing capacities of different steel beams. The yield strength of A36 structural steel is about 36,000 pounds per square inch, and I'd spend hours optimizing the beam placement to ensure the maximum bending moment never exceeded the allowable stress, factoring in a safety margin of course. It's a delicate balance of material efficiency and structural integrity. \n\nMy ex-husband used to complain that I brought my work home too much. He said I was obsessed with numbers and couldn't just relax. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. \n\nMy ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. He said it sounded like a leaky toilet. He actually unplugged it once because he was trying to watch some action movie and said the trickling was distracting. He completely crashed the dissolved oxygen levels in the reservoir. The roots started suffocating within hours because the boundary layer of oxygen around them depleted. I tried to explain the biochemistry of root respiration to him, how without oxygen, the plants switch to anaerobic metabolism and start producing ethanol, which basically poisons them from the inside out. But he just said they were \"just tomatoes\" and I could buy more at the store. He never understood the effort it takes to build a sustainable system. \n\nBut um... sorry, what was the question? Activities I don't enjoy anymore. Yeah, gardening, I guess. I just... I don't have the energy to care about keeping anything alive right now. Not even plants. Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your sleep patterns over the past month?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... yeah, it's been pretty bad. Lately I either lie awake staring at the ceiling replaying bills and work mistakes, or I wake up before dawn feeling like I never actually slept at all. It's just this constant state of exhaustion. \n\nI actually tried to optimize my sleep environment a few weeks ago. I bought these blackout curtains and installed them using heavy-duty anchors because the drywall in my apartment is terrible. It's half-inch gypsum board, which has a pull-out strength of maybe 50 pounds per anchor if you're lucky, but the dynamic load of opening and closing heavy curtains means you really need toggle bolts to distribute the force over a larger surface area behind the wall. Otherwise, the cyclic loading just pulverizes the gypsum core and the screws pull right out. \n\nMy ex-husband never understood that. He tried to hang a massive, heavy mirror in our hallway once using just regular plastic expansion anchors. I told him the shear stress was going to be too much for the drywall without finding a stud, but he just grabbed his drill and said I was overthinking it. Two days later, the whole thing ripped out of the wall and shattered everywhere. It gouged the drywall so badly I had to cut out a whole square, patch it with a new piece of gypsum board, tape the seams, and apply three coats of joint compound, sanding between each one to get a smooth finish. He didn't even help clean up the glass. He just said \"well, it looked fine when I hung it\" and went back to watching TV. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Oh, sleep. Yeah. I just... I can't sleep. I just lie there and think about everything I've messed up, or I wake up at 4 AM and just feel... empty.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Um... no. Not really. I mean, I've tried to think of something, but nothing really feels good anymore. Everything just feels like work. Even eating is just... mechanical. \n\nI did try to make some pasta the other night, just to have something warm. I was boiling the water, and I started thinking about the thermodynamics of the phase change, you know? The latent heat of vaporization for water is about 2260 kilojoules per kilogram, which is incredibly high compared to other common liquids because of the extensive hydrogen bonding between the water molecules. It takes so much thermal energy just to break those bonds before the molecules can actually transition into a gaseous state. \n\nMy ex-husband used to get so impatient waiting for water to boil. He'd always turn the burner up to the absolute maximum, even if he was just heating up a small pot for ramen. I tried to explain to him that once the water reaches 100 degrees Celsius, adding more heat doesn't make it get any hotter or cook the food any faster, it just increases the rate of phase change—it just boils away faster. But he'd just roll his eyes and tell me to stop giving him a physics lecture in the kitchen. He always made me feel like I was being annoying just for understanding how the physical world operates. He actually melted the handle off my favorite saucepan once because the flame was too wide for the base of the pot, and the convective heat transfer just completely warped the phenolic resin of the handle. \n\nBut um... sorry. What did you ask me? Oh, right, pleasure. No, there's nothing. Even the things that used to distract me just feel heavy now. I don't enjoy anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities that you once found enjoyable for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past month?\n\nPatient: Loss of interest? Um... yeah, I guess so. Every day. It's just... I wake up and I just don't care about anything. I don't want to go to work, I don't want to see anyone. It's all just... blank.\n\nI used to really love drafting, you know? Just sitting down with AutoCAD and designing structural supports. I used to get so absorbed in calculating the load-bearing capacities of different steel beams. The yield strength of A36 structural steel is about 36,000 pounds per square inch, and I'd spend hours optimizing the beam placement to ensure the maximum bending moment never exceeded the allowable stress, factoring in a safety margin of course. It's a delicate balance of material efficiency and structural integrity. \n\nMy ex-husband used to complain that I brought my work home too much. He said I was obsessed with numbers and couldn't just relax. We were at a restaurant once, and I was looking at the exposed ceiling, trying to estimate the dead load of the HVAC system hanging from the bar joists. I pointed out that the bridging between the joists looked insufficient to prevent lateral torsional buckling under a full snow load on the roof. He got so mad. He told me to stop analyzing the building and just eat my salad. He said I ruined every date night by turning it into a site inspection. He never appreciated how beautiful it is when a structure is perfectly balanced, how all the forces resolve to zero. He just wanted to drink his beer and talk about his fantasy football team. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Loss of interest? Yeah. I just... I don't even open AutoCAD anymore. I haven't looked at a blueprint in weeks. I just stare at my computer screen and nothing happens. I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is worse in the morning or does it stay fairly consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Worse in the morning? Um... I don't know, it's just... it's just always there. The whole day is just a flat line. I guess waking up is the hardest part because you have to actually face the fact that you have to get through another day of feeling like this. \n\nI remember when mornings used to mean something. I used to get up at 5:30 AM and go for a run, and I'd watch the sun come up over the suspension bridge downtown. It's a really fascinating structure, actually. It uses a continuous truss system, which is incredibly efficient for distributing live loads across multiple spans. The tension cables are anchored deep into the bedrock, and the way they transfer the compressive forces down the main pylons is just a beautiful example of statics. You have to account for thermal expansion, too, especially with the temperature differentials we get here. The expansion joints on the deck can accommodate up to eight inches of movement. \n\nMy ex-husband hated that bridge. He said the tolls were a rip-off and he'd always take the long way around just to save two dollars, even if it cost him more in gas and time. I tried to calculate the fuel consumption of his truck versus the toll cost once, just to show him the math, but he wouldn't listen. He said I was always trying to prove I was smarter than him. He actually got a speeding ticket once on the detour route because he was so mad about the bridge, and then he blamed me for it because I had suggested we just pay the toll. He refused to pay the ticket for months until his license got suspended. \n\nBut um... sorry. The morning. Right. I don't really notice a difference between morning or afternoon anymore. It's all just... heavy. I just don't care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're stuck in place, or the opposite, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't feel restless either. I just... I mostly just sit on the couch. \n\nI was sitting there yesterday, actually, just staring at the coffee table. It's a glass top with a wrought iron base, and I started thinking about the modulus of elasticity of the glass. It's an amorphous solid, so it doesn't have a crystalline structure, which means its fracture mechanics are completely different from metals. When glass breaks, the crack propagation can happen at thousands of meters per second because there are no grain boundaries to arrest the crack tip. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. \n\nMy ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. Well, the original glass top, anyway. He was watching a football game and his team threw an interception, and he just slammed his beer mug down on the table as hard as he could. He didn't even use a coaster. The impact stress exceeded the ultimate tensile strength of the glass and it just shattered everywhere. I tried to explain to him that the localized stress concentration from the heavy base of the mug caused a catastrophic failure, but he just yelled at me for caring more about the table than his team losing. He made me clean up all the glass while he went to the bedroom to sulk. I had to custom order a replacement piece of tempered glass with a beveled edge, and he complained about how much it cost for weeks. \n\nBut um... what did you ask? Moving slowly? No. I just... I don't really move much at all. There's just no point.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Um... didn't you just ask me that? Loss of interest? Yeah, like I said, I just... I don't care about anything anymore. The drafting, the blueprints, all of it. \n\nI used to really enjoy going to the botanical gardens on the weekends. They have this incredible geodesic dome greenhouse. The structural efficiency of a geodesic dome is amazing. It encloses the maximum volume of space with the minimum amount of surface area, and the spherical shape distributes stress perfectly along the triangular framework. Buckminster Fuller really popularized the design, but the actual math behind the structural load distribution is fascinating. The wind load just flows right around it, and the snow load is minimal because of the curvature. \n\nMy ex-husband went with me once, and he hated it. It was incredibly humid inside the dome to support the tropical plants, and he just complained the whole time about sweating through his shirt. I was trying to look at the joint connections where the struts meet at the hubs—they have to be precision-engineered to handle both tension and compression depending on the external forces—and he just kept sighing loudly and asking when we could leave to get burgers. He said looking at plants was like watching paint dry. He completely missed the architectural brilliance of the structure holding the plants. \n\nBut um... yeah. I don't go to the gardens anymore. I don't really do anything that used to bring me pleasure. It's just all... gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Appetite? Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I'm eating normally. I don't really pay attention to it. I just eat whatever's easy, like cereal or toast. 

I was making toast the other day and looking at the toaster. The heating elements are usually made of Nichrome wire, which is an alloy of nickel and chromium. It's perfect for resistive heating because it has a high electrical resistance and it forms a layer of chromium oxide when it's heated for the first time, which prevents it from oxidizing further and burning out. The bimetallic strip that controls the pop-up mechanism is really clever, too. It uses two different metals with different coefficients of thermal expansion bonded together, so as it heats up, one metal expands faster than the other, causing the strip to bend and eventually trigger the release latch. 

My ex-husband was obsessed with making the perfect toast. He had this whole routine where he'd adjust the dial based on the thickness of the bread and the humidity in the air. If it came out even slightly too dark or too light, he'd throw it away and start over, and he'd complain about how the toaster was a piece of junk. I tried to explain to him that the ambient temperature and the voltage fluctuations in the house wiring could affect the heat output of the Nichrome wire, but he just told me to stop being a know-it-all and go buy him a better toaster. I ended up buying this really expensive one with a microchip temperature sensor, and he still complained that it didn't toast evenly. 

But um... what were you asking? Oh, right, my appetite. No, it hasn't changed. I just eat when I have to.

The heating elements are usually made of Nichrome wire, which is an alloy of nickel and chromium. It's perfect for resistive heating because it has a high electrical resistance and it forms a layer of chromium oxide when it's heated for the first time, which prevents it from oxidizing further and burning out. The bimetallic strip that controls the pop-up mechanism is really clever, too. Mild? Um... no. It's definitely not mild. It's... it's ruining everything, honestly. I was making these really questionable calls at work, just completely falling apart in the middle of meetings. My supervisor actually had to step in and take me off all active project decisions. 

I was supposed to be reviewing the soil compaction reports for a new retaining wall. Retaining walls are actually really unforgiving because you have to account for the lateral earth pressure. If the soil behind it gets saturated, the hydrostatic pressure increases exponentially, and you have to design adequate drainage systems, like weep holes, or the overturning moment will cause a total failure. You usually use Rankine's theory to calculate the active earth pressure, assuming the soil is cohesionless, which dictates the sizing of the concrete footing. 

My ex-husband decided to build a small retaining wall in our backyard once to level out a garden bed. He just bought some interlocking blocks from the hardware store and started stacking them right on the dirt. He didn't use any drainage gravel or a proper leveling pad. I tried to explain to him that the heavy clay soil we had would retain water and push the wall over after the first heavy rain, but he just told me I was being condescending and that he didn't need an engineering degree to stack some rocks. Of course, it collapsed two months later after a storm, and he blamed the manufacturer of the blocks. He spent hours on the phone yelling at some poor customer service rep, demanding a refund. 

But um... yeah, work. They took me off all the projects. And at home, I just... I stopped keeping up with basic routines. Like, everything just stopped. I couldn't even manage the bare minimum. It got so bad that I actually ended up being sent to the emergency department recently because... well, I just didn't feel safe with my own thoughts anymore. So... no, it's not mild. It's interfering with everything.

I used to get so absorbed in calculating the load-bearing capacities of different steel beams. The yield strength of A36 structural steel is about 36,000 pounds per square inch, and I'd spend hours optimizing the beam placement to ensure the maximum bending moment never exceeded the allowable stress, factoring in a safety margin of course. It's a delicate balance of material efficiency and structural integrity. \n\nMy ex-husband used to complain that I brought my work home too much. He said I was obsessed with numbers and couldn't just relax. We were at a restaurant once, and I was looking at the exposed ceiling, trying to estimate the dead load of the HVAC system hanging from the bar joists. I pointed out that the bridging between the joists looked insufficient to prevent lateral torsional buckling under a full snow load on the roof. He got so mad. He told me to stop analyzing the building and just eat my salad. He said I ruined every date night by turning it into a site inspection. He never appreciated how beautiful it is when a structure is perfectly balanced, how all the forces resolve to zero. He just wanted to drink his beer and talk about his fantasy football team. \n\nBut um... what were we talking about? Loss of interest? Yeah. I just... I don't even open AutoCAD anymore. I haven't looked at a blueprint in weeks. I just stare at my computer screen and nothing happens. I just don't have it in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is worse in the morning or does it stay fairly consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Worse in the morning? Um... I don't know, it's just... it's just always there. The whole day is just a flat line. I guess waking up is the hardest part because you have to actually face the fact that you have to get through another day of feeling like this. \n\nI remember when mornings used to mean something. I used to get up at 5:30 AM and go for a run, and I'd watch the sun come up over the suspension bridge downtown. It's a really fascinating structure, actually. It uses a continuous truss system, which is incredibly efficient for distributing live loads across multiple spans. The tension cables are anchored deep into the bedrock, and the way they transfer the compressive forces down the main pylons is just a beautiful example of statics. You have to account for thermal expansion, too, especially with the temperature differentials we get here. The expansion joints on the deck can accommodate up to eight inches of movement. \n\nMy ex-husband hated that bridge. He said the tolls were a rip-off and he'd always take the long way around just to save two dollars, even if it cost him more in gas and time. I tried to calculate the fuel consumption of his truck versus the toll cost once, just to show him the math, but he wouldn't listen. He said I was always trying to prove I was smarter than him. He actually got a speeding ticket once on the detour route because he was so mad about the bridge, and then he blamed me for it because I had suggested we just pay the toll. He refused to pay the ticket for months until his license got suspended. \n\nBut um... sorry. The morning. Right. I don't really notice a difference between morning or afternoon anymore. It's all just... heavy. I just don't care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're stuck in place, or the opposite, feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't feel restless either. I just... I mostly just sit on the couch. \n\nI was sitting there yesterday, actually, just staring at the coffee table. It's a glass top with a wrought iron base, and I started thinking about the modulus of elasticity of the glass. It's an amorphous solid, so it doesn't have a crystalline structure, which means its fracture mechanics are completely different from metals. When glass breaks, the crack propagation can happen at thousands of meters per second because there are no grain boundaries to arrest the crack tip. You have to temper it to create internal compressive stresses on the surface, which makes it much stronger against tensile forces. \n\nMy ex-husband actually broke that coffee table once. Well, the original glass top, anyway. He was watching a football game and his team threw an interception, and he just slammed his beer mug down on the table as hard as he could. He didn't even use a coaster. The impact stress exceeded the ultimate tensile strength of the glass and it just shattered everywhere. I tried to explain to him that the localized stress concentration from the heavy base of the mug caused a catastrophic failure, but he just yelled at me for caring more about the table than his team losing. He made me clean up all the glass while he went to the bedroom to sulk. I had to custom order a replacement piece of tempered glass with a beveled edge, and he complained about how much it cost for weeks. \n\nBut um... what did you ask? Moving slowly? No. I just... I don't really move much at all. There's just no point.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Um... didn't you just ask me that? Loss of interest? Yeah, like I said, I just... I don't care about anything anymore. The drafting, the blueprints, all of it. \n\nI used to really enjoy going to the botanical gardens on the weekends. They have this incredible geodesic dome greenhouse. The structural efficiency of a geodesic dome is amazing. It encloses the maximum volume of space with the minimum amount of surface area, and the spherical shape distributes stress perfectly along the triangular framework. Buckminster Fuller really popularized the design, but the actual math behind the structural load distribution is fascinating. The wind load just flows right around it, and the snow load is minimal because of the curvature. \n\nMy ex-husband went with me once, and he hated it. It was incredibly humid inside the dome to support the tropical plants, and he just complained the whole time about sweating through his shirt. He said looking at plants was like watching paint dry. He completely missed the architectural brilliance of the structure holding the plants. \n\nBut um... yeah. I don't go to the gardens anymore. Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I'm eating normally. I don't really pay attention to it. I just eat whatever's easy, like cereal or toast. \n\nI was making toast the other day and looking at the toaster. The heating elements are usually made of Nichrome wire, which is an alloy of nickel and chromium. It's perfect for resistive heating because it has a high electrical resistance and it forms a layer of chromium oxide when it's heated for the first time, which prevents it from oxidizing further and burning out. The bimetallic strip that controls the pop-up mechanism is really clever, too. It uses two different metals with different coefficients of thermal expansion bonded together, so as it heats up, one metal expands faster than the other, causing the strip to bend and eventually trigger the release latch. \n\nMy ex-husband was obsessed with making the perfect toast. He had this whole routine where he'd adjust the dial based on the thickness of the bread and the humidity in the air. If it came out even slightly too dark or too light, he'd throw it away and start over, and he'd complain about how the toaster was a piece of junk. I tried to explain to him that the ambient temperature and the voltage fluctuations in the house wiring could affect the heat output of the Nichrome wire, but he just told me to stop being a know-it-all and go buy him a better toaster. I ended up buying this really expensive one with a microchip temperature sensor, and he still complained that it didn't toast evenly. \n\nBut um... what were you asking? Oh, right, my appetite. No, it hasn't changed. I just eat when I have to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild, or is it interfering significantly with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Mild? Um... no. My supervisor actually had to step in and take me off all active project decisions. \n\nI was supposed to be reviewing the soil compaction reports for a new retaining wall. Retaining walls are actually really unforgiving because you have to account for the lateral earth pressure. If the soil behind it gets saturated, the hydrostatic pressure increases exponentially, and you have to design adequate drainage systems, like weep holes, or the overturning moment will cause a total failure. You usually use Rankine's theory to calculate the active earth pressure, assuming the soil is cohesionless, which dictates the sizing of the concrete footing. \n\nMy ex-husband decided to build a small retaining wall in our backyard once to level out a garden bed. He just bought some interlocking blocks from the hardware store and started stacking them right on the dirt. He didn't use any drainage gravel or a proper leveling pad. I tried to explain to him that the heavy clay soil we had would retain water and push the wall over after the first heavy rain, but he just told me I was being condescending and that he didn't need an engineering degree to stack some rocks. Of course, it collapsed two months later after a storm, and he blamed the manufacturer of the blocks. He spent hours on the phone yelling at some poor customer service rep, demanding a refund. \n\nBut um... yeah, work. They took me off all the projects. And at home, I just... I stopped keeping up with basic routines. Like, everything just stopped. I couldn't even manage the bare minimum.
